And remember the favor of Allah upon you and what has been revealed to you of the Book and wisdom by which He instructs you. And fear Allah and know that Allah is Knowing of all things." (**Sūrat al-Baqarah 2:231**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَإِذَا طَلَّقْتُمُ ٱلنِّسَآءَ فَبَلَغْنَ أَجَلَهُنَّ فَأَمْسِكُوهُنَّ بِمَعْرُوفٍ أَوْ سَرِّحُوهُنَّ بِمَعْرُوفٍ ۚ وَلَا تُمْسِكُوهُنَّ ضِرَارًا لِّتَعْتَدُوا۟ ۚ وَمَن يَفْعَلْ ذَٰلِكَ فَقَدْ ظَلَمَ نَفْسَهُۥ ۚ وَلَا تَتَّخِذُوٓا۟ ءَايَـٰتِ ٱللَّهِ هُزُوًا ۚ وَٱذْكُرُوا۟ نِعْمَتَ ٱللَّهِ عَلَيْكُمْ وَمَآ أَنزَلَ عَلَيْكُم مِّنَ ٱلْكِتَـٰبِ وَٱلْحِكْمَةِ يَعِظُكُم بِهِۦ ۚ وَٱتَّقُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ وَٱعْلَمُوٓا۟ أَنَّ ٱللَّهَ بِكُلِّ شَىْءٍ عَلِيمٌ ۝٢٣١﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ البَقَرَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. A Qur’anic chapter opens by affirming that God heard the complaint of a woman who came disputing with Prophet Muhammad ﷺ about her husband, placing an ordinary woman’s grievance within the scripture itself.

   > "Indeed, Allah has heard the argument of the woman who pleaded with you ˹O Prophet˺ concerning her husband, and appealed to Allah. Allah has heard your exchange. Surely Allah is All-Hearing, All-Seeing." (**Sūrat al-Mujādilah 58:1**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿قَدْ سَمِعَ ٱللَّهُ قَوْلَ ٱلَّتِى تُجَـٰدِلُكَ فِى زَوْجِهَا وَتَشْتَكِىٓ إِلَى ٱللَّهِ وَٱللَّهُ يَسْمَعُ تَحَاوُرَكُمَآ ۚ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ سَمِيعٌۢ بَصِيرٌ ۝١﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ المُجَادلَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ taught that ordinary tenderness and financial care within marriage can be rewarded, even down to placing a morsel of food in one’s wife’s mouth with sincere intention.

   > "Whatever you spend seeking God's pleasure is rewarded, even the morsel you place in your wife's mouth." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 5354[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ explicitly told men not to prevent women from attending communal worship.

   > "Do not prevent the female servants of God from the mosques of God." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 900[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an requires men to live honorably with their wives even when affection is strained, so decent treatment is not made conditional on ideal feelings.

   > "O you who have believed, it is not lawful for you to inherit women by compulsion. And do not make difficulties for them in order to take [back] part of what you gave them unless they commit a clear immorality. And live with them in kindness. For if you dislike them - perhaps you dislike a thing and Allah makes therein much good." (**Sūrat al-Nisāʾ 4:19**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿يَـٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ لَا يَحِلُّ لَكُمْ أَن تَرِثُوا۟ ٱلنِّسَآءَ كَرْهًا ۖ وَلَا تَعْضُلُوهُنَّ لِتَذْهَبُوا۟ بِبَعْضِ مَآ ءَاتَيْتُمُوهُنَّ إِلَّآ أَن يَأْتِينَ بِفَـٰحِشَةٍ مُّبَيِّنَةٍ ۚ وَعَاشِرُوهُنَّ بِٱلْمَعْرُوفِ ۚ فَإِن كَرِهْتُمُوهُنَّ فَعَسَىٰٓ أَن تَكْرَهُوا۟ شَيْـًٔا وَيَجْعَلَ ٱللَّهُ فِيهِ خَيْرًا كَثِيرًا ۝١٩﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ النِّسَاءِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. When Safiyya came to Prophet Muhammad ﷺ crying after being insulted over her Jewish ancestry, he comforted her by affirming the prophetic lineage of her people and rebuked the insult.

   > "You are the daughter of a prophet, your uncle is a prophet, and you are married to a prophet. So what can she boast over you about?" — *Jami at-Tirmidhi* 3894[5](#ref-5){: .footnote}[16](#ref-16){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. When a man sought to join voluntary fighting while his parents were still living, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ redirected him to serve and care for them instead.

   > "Are your parents alive?" He said, "Yes." He replied, "Then strive in their service." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 5972[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. After women complained that their husbands had beaten them, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ said that the men who did so were not the best among the Muslim community. The report preserves a difficult legal sequence, but his moral judgment on the resulting conduct is explicit.

   > "Many women have gone around Muhammad's family complaining of their husbands. Those men are not the best among you." — *Sunan Abi Dawud* 2146[4](#ref-4){: .footnote}[15](#ref-15){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. When a father asked Prophet Muhammad ﷺ to witness a gift he had given one child but not the others, he refused and instructed parents to treat their children fairly.

   > "Be conscious of God and be just among your children." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2587[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. When Prophet Muhammad ﷺ personally encouraged Barirah to return to her former husband, she asked whether he was commanding her. He said he was only interceding; she declined, and he accepted her decision.

   > "Are you ordering me?" He said, "No, I am only interceding." She said, "I have no need of him." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 5283[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Aisha remembered small signs of affection in their marriage, including Prophet Muhammad ﷺ deliberately drinking from the same place on a cup where she had just drunk.

   > "I would drink... then hand it to the Prophet ﷺ, and he would place his mouth where mine had been." — *Sahih Muslim* 300[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}[54](#ref-54){: .footnote}[55](#ref-55){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Anas reported that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ used to greet children when he passed them, treating them as people worthy of direct attention rather than ignoring them as beneath adult notice.

   > "Anas passed by a group of boys and greeted them, then said: 'The Prophet ﷺ used to do so.'" — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 6247[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

---

## <span class="num-roman">II.</span> The man who lived the teachings
{: #the-man-who-lived-the-teachings }

A teacher’s moral claims are easier to assess when we can compare them with his conduct away from formal teaching. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was observed over many years by his wives, a young household servant, close friends, political opponents, poor petitioners, soldiers, and strangers. Their reports are particularly valuable when they describe ordinary behavior or moments in which he was under pressure.

### <span class="num-alpha">A.</span> What people closest to him actually saw
{: #what-people-closest-to-him-actually-saw }

1. When Prophet Muhammad ﷺ returned frightened from the first revelation, Khadija reassured him by recalling the character she had already observed in him: maintaining family ties, helping the needy, honoring guests, and supporting people in hardship.

   > "By God, God will never disgrace you. You keep family ties, carry the burden of the weak, help the destitute, honor the guest, and assist those struck by calamity." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Anas, who served Prophet Muhammad ﷺ for ten years from a young age, remembered that he was not habitually scolded or blamed even during years of ordinary daily contact.

   > "I served the Prophet ﷺ for ten years. He never said 'uff' to me and never said, 'Why did you do that?' or 'Why did you not do that?'" — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 6038[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. On one occasion, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ sent the young Anas on an errand and later found him distracted and playing; he smiled and gently reminded him of the task rather than humiliating him.

   > He came up smiling and said, "Little Anas, did you go where I asked you to go?" — *Sahih Muslim* 2310a[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Aisha, speaking from within Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ household, reported that he never struck a woman or a servant.

   > "The Messenger of God ﷺ never struck anyone with his hand, neither a woman nor a servant, except in battle." — *Sahih Muslim* 2328a[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Aisha also reported that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ did not take revenge for wrongs done to him personally, while distinguishing those personal injuries from matters of public justice.

   > "The Messenger of God ﷺ never took revenge for himself." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3560[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Aisha said that when Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was given a choice between two morally permissible options, he chose the easier one unless it involved wrongdoing.

   > "Whenever the Messenger of God ﷺ was given a choice between two matters, he chose the easier one so long as it was not sinful." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3560[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Anas remembered Prophet Muhammad ﷺ as both generous and courageous; among the concrete examples preserved is the night when a frightening sound was heard in Medina and Prophet Muhammad ﷺ rode toward the danger before others.

   > "The Prophet ﷺ was the best in character, the most generous of people, and the bravest of people." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 6033[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an tells Prophet Muhammad ﷺ that his gentleness helped keep people around him and that harshness or hard-heartedness would have driven them away.

   > "It is out of Allah’s mercy that you ˹O Prophet˺ have been lenient with them. Had you been cruel or hard-hearted, they would have certainly abandoned you. So pardon them, ask Allah’s forgiveness for them, and consult with them in ˹conducting˺ matters. Once you make a decision, put your trust in Allah. Surely Allah loves those who trust in Him." (**Sūrat Āl ʿImrān 3:159**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿فَبِمَا رَحْمَةٍ مِّنَ ٱللَّهِ لِنتَ لَهُمْ ۖ وَلَوْ كُنتَ فَظًّا غَلِيظَ ٱلْقَلْبِ لَٱنفَضُّوا۟ مِنْ حَوْلِكَ ۖ فَٱعْفُ عَنْهُمْ وَٱسْتَغْفِرْ لَهُمْ وَشَاوِرْهُمْ فِى ٱلْأَمْرِ ۖ فَإِذَا عَزَمْتَ فَتَوَكَّلْ عَلَى ٱللَّهِ ۚ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ يُحِبُّ ٱلْمُتَوَكِّلِينَ ۝١٥٩﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ آلِ عِمۡرَانَ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. When Aisha was asked about Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ character, she said that his character was the Qur’an, linking the scripture he taught publicly with the conduct she observed privately.

   > "His character was the Qur’an." — *Sahih Muslim* 746a[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

### <span class="num-alpha">B.</span> Mercy when he had reason to be angry
{: #mercy-when-he-had-reason-to-be-angry }

1. A desert Arab once yanked Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ cloak hard enough to leave a mark and then demanded money. Although Prophet Muhammad ﷺ had authority and companions around him, he turned toward the man, smiled, and ordered that he be given something.

   > "The Prophet ﷺ turned toward him, smiled, and ordered that a gift be given to him." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3149[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. When a stranger urinated inside the mosque, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ stopped the crowd from attacking him, let the man finish, had the area washed, and then explained the rule without unnecessary humiliation.

   > "Leave him... You were sent to make things easy, not to make them difficult." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 6128[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. While Prophet Muhammad ﷺ slept during a journey, a man stood over him with his unsheathed sword and asked who would protect him. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ answered, “God,” and after the immediate danger passed the report says he did not punish the man.

   > "He said, 'Who will protect you from me?' I said, 'God'... The Prophet ﷺ did not punish him." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2910[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. After a man insulted Prophet Muhammad ﷺ over the distribution of wealth, he recalled that Moses had endured worse treatment and chose patience rather than retaliating for the personal insult.

   > "May God have mercy on Moses. He was hurt with more than this, yet he was patient." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4335[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. When Tufayl ibn Amr asked Prophet Muhammad ﷺ to invoke God against the tribe of Daws for rejecting his message, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ instead prayed for their guidance.

   > "O God, guide Daws and bring them." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2937[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. When Abdullah ibn Ubayy, a major internal opponent in Medina, died, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ gave his own shirt for the man’s shroud and prayed over him at the son’s request. Qur’an 9:84 then prohibited him from doing so in such cases thereafter, preserving both his initial inclination toward mercy and his submission to a claimed revelation that restricted it.

   > His son asked for Muhammad’s ﷺ shirt and funeral prayer, "so the Prophet ﷺ gave him his shirt" and went to pray for him. — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 1269[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. When companions asked Prophet Muhammad ﷺ to curse hostile polytheists, he refused to define his mission in those terms and said that he had been sent as a mercy rather than as a habitual curser.

   > "I was not sent as one who curses. I was sent only as a mercy." — *Sahih Muslim* 2599[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. After repeated political harm from Abdullah ibn Ubayy, Umar asked permission to kill him. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ refused, in part because he did not want to become known as a leader who killed people regarded as members of his own community.

   > "Leave him, lest people say Muhammad kills his companions." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4905[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ survived poisoned food served to him at Khaybar and later said during his final illness that he still felt its effects. Reports differ over what ultimately happened to the woman responsible after another man died from the same food, but neither version presents punishment as personal revenge for Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ own injury.

   > During his final illness he said that he still felt the effect of the food he had eaten at Khaybar. — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4428[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sahih al-Bukhari* 5777[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

### <span class="num-alpha">C.</span> Humility and a strikingly unroyal life
{: #humility-and-a-strikingly-unroyal-life }

1. Even while leading the Muslim community, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ continued to help with ordinary household work at home.

   > "He used to serve his family." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 676[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Aisha reported that Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ household sometimes went for roughly two months without lighting a cooking fire, living mainly on dates and water with milk supplied by neighbors.

   > "We would see three crescents in two months, and no fire would be lit in the houses of the Messenger of God ﷺ." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 6459[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ died while his armor was pledged to a Jewish merchant in exchange for barley, despite having led Medina and controlled substantial public resources.

   > "The Messenger of God ﷺ died while his iron armor was pledged to a Jewish man for thirty measures of barley." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2916[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ sometimes slept on bedding so coarse that it left marks on his body; Umar wept when he saw them and compared his circumstances with those of the Byzantine and Persian rulers.

   > Umar said, "Caesar and Khosrau live as they do, while you are the Messenger of God." He replied, "Are you not content that they have this world and we the next?" — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4913[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ sat among his companions without obvious royal ceremony, sometimes leaving strangers unable to identify which man he was, and his companions reported that he disliked people routinely standing for him when he arrived.

   > "Which of you is Muhammad?" — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 63; see also *Jami al-Tirmidhi* 2754, graded *hasan* [good][2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[5](#ref-5){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[16](#ref-16){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Companions reported that Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ displeasure was often visible on his face without becoming coarse or humiliating speech toward the person involved.

   > "The Prophet ﷺ was more modest than a virgin in her private room." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3562, 6102; *Sahih Muslim* 2320[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

### <span class="num-alpha">D.</span> Generosity and courage when they cost something
{: #generosity-and-courage-when-they-cost-something }

1. Ibn Abbas reported that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ became especially generous during Ramadan, comparing the speed and openness of his giving to a freely blowing wind.

   > "The Messenger of God ﷺ was the most generous of people, and he was most generous in Ramadan." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 6[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Anas reported that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ once gave a man a flock large enough to fill the space between two mountains, leading the recipient to tell his people that Muhammad ﷺ gave as someone who did not fear poverty.

   > "Muhammad gives like a man who does not fear poverty." — *Sahih Muslim* 2312a[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. After the battle of Hunayn, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ continued giving material support to Safwan ibn Umayya while Safwan still disliked him; Safwan later said that repeated generosity changed his feelings until Prophet Muhammad ﷺ became the person he loved most.

   > "He continued giving to me until he became the dearest of people to me, though before that he had been the most hateful of people to me." — *Sahih Muslim* 2313[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. When a frightening sound alarmed Medina one night, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ rode alone toward the source on an unsaddled horse before the others had finished gathering to investigate.

   > They met him returning, a sword at his neck, saying, "Do not be afraid. Do not be afraid." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 6033[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. At Hunayn, when a sudden attack caused part of the Muslim force to retreat, al-Bara reported that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ himself remained in place on his mule rather than fleeing with them.

   > Al-Bara said, "The Messenger of God ﷺ did not flee." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4317[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. During one journey, Bedouins crowded Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, pulled away his cloak, and demanded shares of wealth. He did not retaliate and instead told them how freely he would distribute wealth if he possessed enough of it.

   > "If I had camels as numerous as these thorn trees, I would distribute them among you. You would not find me miserly, false, or cowardly." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2821[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ told Abu Dharr that even if he possessed gold equal to Mount Uhud, he would not want to retain it for more than a few nights except what was needed to pay a debt.

   > "I would not like to have gold equal to this Mount Uhud unless nothing of it remained with me after three nights, except something I kept for a debt." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 6445[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ shared the physical danger faced by his followers in battle and was himself wounded in the face at Uhud.

   > "On that day, the Prophet’s face was wounded and one of his teeth was broken." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2911; *Sahih Muslim* 1790[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

   Another Bukhari account adds that pieces of his helmet were driven into his cheek. The bodily danger of the battlefield reached him as it reached the people he led. — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4075[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}

1. Anas remembered Prophet Muhammad ﷺ as someone who ordinarily did not give a flat refusal when asked for something he was able to provide; read alongside reports of his sparse household life, the pattern is of resources passing through his hands rather than being accumulated privately.

   > "The Prophet ﷺ was never asked for something to be given for which his answer was 'no.'" — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 6034[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

### <span class="num-alpha">E.</span> Tenderness, loyalty, grief, and human warmth
{: #tenderness-loyalty-grief-and-human-warmth }

1. When Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ infant son Ibrahim died, he wept and explained that tears and grief are compatible with patient submission to God.

   > "The eyes shed tears and the heart grieves... O Ibrahim, we are truly grieved by your departure." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 1303[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Years after Khadija’s death, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ continued speaking warmly of her and sending gifts or food to her friends.

   > "When he slaughtered a sheep, he would send portions to the friends of Khadija." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 6004[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Aisha remembered racing Prophet Muhammad ﷺ for fun, preserving an ordinary example of humor and companionship within their marriage.

   > After each had won one race, he said with humor, "This one is for that one." — *Sunan Abi Dawud* 2578[4](#ref-4){: .footnote}[15](#ref-15){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Anas reported that when a socially vulnerable woman with an intellectual or mental difficulty asked for Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ attention, he allowed her to choose where they would speak and remained with her until her need was met.

   > She said, "Messenger of God, I have a need from you." He told her to choose a side of the road, and he stayed with her until her need was met. — *Sahih Muslim* 2326[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. After Prophet Muhammad ﷺ drank from a vessel, a boy on his right had the customary right to receive it next while older men sat on his left. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ asked the boy’s permission to serve the elders first; when the boy declined, he honored the boy’s turn.

   > He asked the boy, "Will you permit me to give these elders first?" ... Then the Messenger of God ﷺ placed the cup in the boy’s hand. — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2602[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Abu Huraira reported that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ did not insult food he disliked; he ate it if he wanted it and otherwise left it without disparaging it.

   > "The Prophet ﷺ never criticized food. If he wanted it, he ate it; otherwise, he left it." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3563[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. In one report, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ recited the prayers of earlier prophets for their peoples, then raised his hands and wept out of concern for what would become of his own community before God.

   > He raised his hands and said, "O Lord, my community, my community," and wept. — *Sahih Muslim* 202[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Anas remembered Prophet Muhammad ﷺ noticing the grief of his little brother Abu Umayr after the child’s pet bird died and gently speaking to him about it.

   > "O Abu Umayr, what happened to the little bird?" — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 6129[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Aisha reported that when Fatima visited Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, he would rise to welcome her, take her hand, kiss her, and seat her in his own place; Fatima treated him similarly when he visited her.

   > "When she entered upon him, he stood for her, took her hand, kissed her, and seated her where he had been sitting." — *Sunan Abi Dawud* 5217[4](#ref-4){: .footnote}[15](#ref-15){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. When a poor Black man or woman who regularly cleaned the mosque died and was buried without Prophet Muhammad ﷺ being told, he noticed the person’s absence, asked what had happened, went to the grave, and prayed there.

   > "Why did you not inform me?" Then he went to the grave and prayed over it. — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 458[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Jarir ibn Abdullah reported that after he became Muslim, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ did not shut him out and regularly greeted him with a smile.

   > "Whenever he saw me, he received me with a smile." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3822[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Companions reported that Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ speech was not obscene or deliberately coarse, matching the restraint in language he expected from others.

   > "The Prophet ﷺ was neither obscene nor one who deliberately spoke obscenely... The best of you are those best in character." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 6029[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Aisha remembered Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ speech as measured and distinct enough for listeners to understand rather than rushed and difficult to follow.

   > "He did not speak rapidly as you do. He spoke with clear words, separated from one another." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3568[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

   Anas likewise remembered that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ sometimes repeated an important statement three times so that listeners would understand it. He took responsibility for making himself clear rather than assuming that the audience would keep up. — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 95[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}

1. When Aisha wanted to watch an Abyssinian display in the mosque, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ screened her with his cloak and remained there until she had watched for as long as she wished.

   > "The Prophet ﷺ screened me with his cloak while I watched the Abyssinians playing... until I had enough." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 5236[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Aisha once gave three dates to a poor mother with two daughters. The woman gave one to each child and divided her own date between them; when Aisha told Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, he used the incident to teach the religious value of caring generously for daughters.

   > "Whoever is tested with daughters and treats them generously, they will be a shield for him from the Fire." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 5995[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. After a battle, when the companions listed those who had died but omitted the socially obscure Julaybib, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ noticed that he was missing and told them to search for him.

   > "But I am missing Julaybib. Search for him." ... "He is of me and I am of him." — *Sahih Muslim* 2472[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ joked with companions and children but taught that humor did not justify saying things that were false.

   > "I do not say anything except the truth." — *Jami al-Tirmidhi* 1990, graded *hasan* [good][5](#ref-5){: .footnote}[16](#ref-16){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. When Prophet Muhammad ﷺ wept over another dying child, he described the tears as mercy, treating tenderness toward another person’s suffering as compatible with strength and faith.

   > "This is mercy God has placed in the hearts of His servants, and God shows mercy to the merciful." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 7377[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. At a graveyard, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ told his companions that he longed to meet believers who would come after his lifetime, calling those later believers his “brothers.”

   > "I would love to see my brothers... They are those who have not yet come." — *Sahih Muslim* 249a[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

---

## <span class="num-roman">III.</span> A life tested by marginalization, persecution, authority, and victory
{: #a-life-tested-by-marginalization-persecution-authority-and-victory }

Prophet Muhammad ﷺ lived long enough after claiming prophethood for his character to be tested under opposite conditions. He spent years unable to protect even his own followers, then later became the leader of a state and victor over the city that had expelled him. The contrast makes the chronology morally important, not merely biographical.[8](#ref-8){: .footnote}[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}[41](#ref-41){: .footnote}[42](#ref-42){: .footnote}

### <span class="num-alpha">A.</span> Before revelation: an orphan, worker, husband, and trusted arbitrator
{: #before-revelation-an-orphan-worker-husband-and-trusted-arbitrator }

1. According to early biography, Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ father died before he was born and his mother died while he was still a child. The Qur’an later reminds him that he had been an orphan and immediately turns that memory into commands not to mistreat orphans or turn away people who ask for help.

   > "Did He not find you an orphan and give [you] refuge?" (**Sūrat ad-Duḥā 93:6**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿أَلَمْ يَجِدْكَ يَتِيمًا فَـَٔاوَىٰ ۝٦﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الضُّحَىٰ</span></div>
   >
   > "So as for the orphan, do not oppress [him]. And as for the petitioner, do not repel [him]." (**Sūrat ad-Duḥā 93:9-10**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿فَأَمَّا ٱلْيَتِيمَ فَلَا تَقْهَرْ ۝٩ وَأَمَّا ٱلسَّآئِلَ فَلَا تَنْهَرْ ۝١٠﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الضُّحَىٰ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Before leading a community, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ earned his living by tending sheep and later working in trade, so his later teaching about lawful work came from someone who had himself worked for a living.

   > Asked whether he had tended sheep, he said, "Yes. I used to tend the sheep of the people of Mecca for small coins." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2262[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Early biography remembers Prophet Muhammad ﷺ as a trusted arbitrator before his prophetic claim. During the rebuilding of the Kaaba, when the Meccan clans nearly fought over who would replace the Black Stone, he proposed that representatives lift it together on a cloth before he set it in place.

   > The clans agreed that the next man to enter should arbitrate; when Muhammad came, they accepted him, and his solution shared the honor among them. — *Sīra*: Ibn Ishaq, trans. A. Guillaume, pp. 84–86[8](#ref-8){: .footnote}[19](#ref-19){: .footnote}[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. According to the canonical account, Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ prophetic experience began around age forty while he was withdrawing for periods of worship in the cave of Hira, not from an existing position of political power.

   > "The love of seclusion was given to him. He would go to the cave of Hira and devote himself to worship there for a number of nights." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The canonical account describes Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ first response to revelation as fear: he returned to Khadija trembling and asked to be covered, rather than publicly announcing the event as a triumph.

   > He returned to Khadija "with his heart trembling" and said, "Cover me, cover me." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Khadija reassured Prophet Muhammad ﷺ after the first revelation by listing qualities she had already observed in him—maintaining family ties, helping the weak, generosity, hospitality, and support in hardship—before taking him to Waraqa ibn Nawfal.

   > "God will never disgrace you. You keep family ties, support the weak, help the destitute, honor guests, and assist those afflicted." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Waraqa told Prophet Muhammad ﷺ that the experience resembled the revelation given to Moses and warned that his own people would eventually drive him out; Prophet Muhammad ﷺ reacted with surprise at the prospect of expulsion.

   > Muhammad ﷺ asked, "Will they drive me out?" Waraqa replied that no one had brought a message like this without being opposed. — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Early biography records Prophet Muhammad ﷺ participating before prophethood in a Meccan alliance whose members pledged to restore the rights of people who had been wronged, placing concern for justice before his later political authority.

   > "They pledged that they would stand with any wronged person in Mecca until his right was restored." — *Sīra*: Ibn Hisham, *al-Sīrah al-Nabawiyyah* 1/133[8](#ref-8){: .footnote}[19](#ref-19){: .footnote}[20](#ref-20){: .footnote}[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[29](#ref-29){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Early biography describes Khadija as a merchant who employed Prophet Muhammad ﷺ in trade before marrying him after observing his conduct. They remained married for roughly twenty-five years, and although plural marriage was socially available, he did not marry another woman while she was alive.

   > "The Messenger of God ﷺ did not marry another woman until Khadija died." — *Sahih Muslim* 2436[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sīra*: Ibn Ishaq/Ibn Hisham, for the marriage narrative[8](#ref-8){: .footnote}[19](#ref-19){: .footnote}[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The name *Muhammad* means “much praised” or “repeatedly praised.” He bore the name before making a prophetic claim, endured years of mockery during his mission, and later became a person whose name is invoked daily by Muslims around the world. The correspondence is notable but does not by itself establish prophethood.[77](#ref-77){: .footnote}[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}

1. The sources do not describe Prophet Muhammad ﷺ spending his first forty years as a poet, priest, scripture scholar, or professional religious speaker. His prophetic claim appeared after a long ordinary adult life, and the Qur’an itself appeals to that earlier life before the audience who had known him.

   > "You did not know what is the Book or [what is] faith, but We have made it a light by which We guide whom We will of Our servants." (**Sūrat ash-Shūrā 42:52**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}; compare **Sūrat al-‘Ankabūt 29:48**[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَكَذَٰلِكَ أَوْحَيْنَآ إِلَيْكَ رُوحًا مِّنْ أَمْرِنَا ۚ مَا كُنتَ تَدْرِى مَا ٱلْكِتَـٰبُ وَلَا ٱلْإِيمَـٰنُ وَلَـٰكِن جَعَلْنَـٰهُ نُورًا نَّهْدِى بِهِۦ مَن نَّشَآءُ مِنْ عِبَادِنَا ۚ وَإِنَّكَ لَتَهْدِىٓ إِلَىٰ صِرَٰطٍ مُّسْتَقِيمٍ ۝٥٢﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الشُّورَىٰ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

### <span class="num-alpha">B.</span> Mecca: choosing the message at great personal cost
{: #mecca-choosing-the-message-at-great-personal-cost }

1. Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ early public message challenged major features of Meccan religious and social life: it called people away from inherited idols to worship one God and condemned neglect of the poor, cheating, arrogance, and exploitation.

   > "Say, 'He is Allah, [who is] One,'" (**Sūrat al-Ikhlāṣ 112:1**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿قُلْ هُوَ ٱللَّهُ أَحَدٌ ۝١﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الإِخۡلَاصِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an instructed Prophet Muhammad ﷺ to begin his public warning with his own close relatives rather than exempting his clan from the message’s moral demands.

   > "And warn, [O Muhammad], your closest kindred." (**Sūrat ash-Shu'arā' 26:214**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَأَنذِرْ عَشِيرَتَكَ ٱلْأَقْرَبِينَ ۝٢١٤﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الشُّعَرَاءِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Khabbab ibn al-Aratt later recalled complaining about persecution while the Muslims had no political power. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ answered by calling them to patience and confidence that their circumstances would eventually change, not by promising immediate relief or authorizing private revenge.

   > "By God, God will complete this matter until a traveler goes safely... but you are being hasty." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3612[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ did not compromise the central claim of monotheism in order to reach a religious settlement with Meccan polytheism; a short Qur’anic chapter draws a direct boundary between the two forms of worship.

   > "For you is your religion, and for me is my religion." (**Sūrat al-Kāfirūn 109:6**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿لَكُمْ دِينُكُمْ وَلِىَ دِينِ ۝٦﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الكَافِرُونَ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an records pressure on Prophet Muhammad ﷺ to compromise with his opponents and explicitly warns him not to yield, restricting his freedom to reshape the message for political convenience.

   > "They wish that you would soften [in your position], so they would soften [toward you]." (**Sūrat al-Qalam 68:9**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَدُّوا۟ لَوْ تُدْهِنُ فَيُدْهِنُونَ ۝٩﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ القَلَمِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Late in the Meccan period, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ lost both Khadija, his closest emotional supporter, and Abu Talib, the uncle whose clan protection had been politically important. He continued preaching after both sources of support were gone.

   > "God will never disgrace you," Khadija had told him at the beginning of the mission. — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sīra*: Ibn Ishaq, for the chronology of the Meccan losses[8](#ref-8){: .footnote}[19](#ref-19){: .footnote}[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

   The bereavements continued beyond that year. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ outlived all of his children except Fatima, and she survived him by only about six months. His prophetic life was never insulated from ordinary human grief.

1. Bukhari’s account of Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ rejection at al-Ta’if records his deep distress and his refusal of an angelic offer to crush the people who had rejected him, choosing instead to hope for good from their descendants.

   > "Rather, I hope that God will bring from their descendants people who worship God alone." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3231[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. When Prophet Muhammad ﷺ finally left Mecca, he did so as a hunted migrant. The Qur’an recalls him hiding with Abu Bakr in a cave while pursuers searched for them.

   > "˹It does not matter˺ if you ˹believers˺ do not support him, for Allah did in fact support him when the disbelievers drove him out ˹of Mecca˺ and he was only one of two. While they both were in the cave, he reassured his companion, 'Do not worry; Allah is certainly with us.' So Allah sent down His serenity upon the Prophet, supported him with forces you ˹believers˺ did not see, and made the word of the disbelievers lowest, while the Word of Allah is supreme. And Allah is Almighty, All-Wise." (**Sūrat at-Tawbah 9:40**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿إِلَّا تَنصُرُوهُ فَقَدْ نَصَرَهُ ٱللَّهُ إِذْ أَخْرَجَهُ ٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟ ثَانِىَ ٱثْنَيْنِ إِذْ هُمَا فِى ٱلْغَارِ إِذْ يَقُولُ لِصَـٰحِبِهِۦ لَا تَحْزَنْ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ مَعَنَا ۖ فَأَنزَلَ ٱللَّهُ سَكِينَتَهُۥ عَلَيْهِ وَأَيَّدَهُۥ بِجُنُودٍ لَّمْ تَرَوْهَا وَجَعَلَ كَلِمَةَ ٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟ ٱلسُّفْلَىٰ ۗ وَكَلِمَةُ ٱللَّهِ هِىَ ٱلْعُلْيَا ۗ وَٱللَّهُ عَزِيزٌ حَكِيمٌ ۝٤٠﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ التَّوۡبَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. During the migration, Suraqa pursued Prophet Muhammad ﷺ and Abu Bakr in an attempt to capture them. After failing, he asked for safety, and the hadith account says Prophet Muhammad ﷺ granted it and asked him not to disclose their route.

   > Suraqa asked for a written guarantee of security, and the Prophet ﷺ ordered that one be written for him. — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3906[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. During the years when the Muslims lacked political power, the message repeatedly emphasized patience and restraint rather than treating mockery or injury as permission for individuals to begin cycles of revenge.

   > "And be patient over what they say and avoid them with gracious avoidance." (**Sūrat al-Muzzammil 73:10**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَٱصْبِرْ عَلَىٰ مَا يَقُولُونَ وَٱهْجُرْهُمْ هَجْرًا جَمِيلًا ۝١٠﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ المُزَّمِّلِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an forbade Prophet Muhammad ﷺ from dismissing sincere low-status worshippers in order to make his circle more attractive to social elites.

   > "And do not send away those who call upon their Lord morning and afternoon, seeking His countenance." (**Sūrat al-An'ām 6:52**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَلَا تَطْرُدِ ٱلَّذِينَ يَدْعُونَ رَبَّهُم بِٱلْغَدَوٰةِ وَٱلْعَشِىِّ يُرِيدُونَ وَجْهَهُۥ ۖ مَا عَلَيْكَ مِنْ حِسَابِهِم مِّن شَىْءٍ وَمَا مِنْ حِسَابِكَ عَلَيْهِم مِّن شَىْءٍ فَتَطْرُدَهُمْ فَتَكُونَ مِنَ ٱلظَّـٰلِمِينَ ۝٥٢﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الأَنۡعَامِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Before the Muslims had a state of their own, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ sent persecuted followers to seek refuge under the Christian ruler of Abyssinia because he was known for justice.

   > "There is a king there under whom no one is wronged." — *Sīra*: Ibn Ishaq, preserved by Ibn Hisham, report of the first migration to Abyssinia[8](#ref-8){: .footnote}[19](#ref-19){: .footnote}[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Early biography records Prophet Muhammad ﷺ and his clan enduring a prolonged social and economic boycott while he continued preaching rather than abandoning or diluting the message.

   > "Do the people think that they will be left to say, 'We believe' and they will not be tried?" (**Sūrat al-'Ankabūt 29:2**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿أَحَسِبَ ٱلنَّاسُ أَن يُتْرَكُوٓا۟ أَن يَقُولُوٓا۟ ءَامَنَّا وَهُمْ لَا يُفْتَنُونَ ۝٢﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ العَنكَبُوتِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The migration was carefully planned with routes, provisions, information, concealment, and the assistance of a skilled non-Muslim guide, showing reliance on God alongside ordinary practical preparation and trust in competent expertise across a religious boundary.

   > "The Prophet ﷺ and Abu Bakr hired a skilled guide... who followed the religion of Quraysh." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2264; see also Qur'an 9:40[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Early biography reports that Meccan leaders offered Prophet Muhammad ﷺ wealth, leadership or kingship, marriage, and treatment if he would stop preaching, and that he refused. Because the episode comes from *sira* [early biography] rather than Bukhari or Muslim, it should be weighed at that level of evidence, but as preserved it makes the material cost of continuing his mission especially clear.

   > The early sira preserves negotiations in which Quraysh offered wealth and leadership if Muhammad ﷺ would abandon the message. — *Sīra*: Ibn Ishaq, as preserved by Ibn Hisham[8](#ref-8){: .footnote}[19](#ref-19){: .footnote}[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Bukhari records public physical assaults on Prophet Muhammad ﷺ during the Meccan years, including enemies placing slaughter remains on him while he prostrated and another man tightening a cloth around his neck while he prayed, at a time when he had no state through which to punish the attackers.

   > Reported in *Sahih al-Bukhari* 240[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote} and *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3856[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}.
   {: .quote }

1. Early biography reports that even as Meccan opponents plotted Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ death, some still had property deposited with him for safekeeping, and that he arranged for Ali to remain behind and return those trusts. If accepted, the report shows that hostility to his religious claim had not erased confidence in his ordinary honesty.

   > Reported in the *Sīra* of Ibn Ishaq/Ibn Hisham, in the account of the migration[8](#ref-8){: .footnote}[19](#ref-19){: .footnote}[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}.
   {: .quote }

### <span class="num-alpha">C.</span> Medina: community, conflict, restraint, and the test of power
{: #medina-community-conflict-restraint-and-the-test-of-power }

1. The migration to Medina in 622 changed Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ position from a persecuted preacher to the leader of an emerging multi-clan community, allowing his conduct under authority to be compared with the moral standards he had taught while politically weak.

   > "And [also for] those who were settled in al-Madinah and [adopted] the faith before them. They love those who emigrated to them and find not any want in their breasts of what the emigrants were given but give [them] preference over themselves, even though they are in privation." (**Sūrat al-Ḥashr 59:9**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَٱلَّذِينَ تَبَوَّءُو ٱلدَّارَ وَٱلْإِيمَـٰنَ مِن قَبْلِهِمْ يُحِبُّونَ مَنْ هَاجَرَ إِلَيْهِمْ وَلَا يَجِدُونَ فِى صُدُورِهِمْ حَاجَةً مِّمَّآ أُوتُوا۟ وَيُؤْثِرُونَ عَلَىٰٓ أَنفُسِهِمْ وَلَوْ كَانَ بِهِمْ خَصَاصَةٌ ۚ وَمَن يُوقَ شُحَّ نَفْسِهِۦ فَأُو۟لَـٰٓئِكَ هُمُ ٱلْمُفْلِحُونَ ۝٩﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الحَشۡرِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The early Medinan charter records a political compact that included Jewish groups while recognizing their separate religion. Because the document is preserved through early biography and historical works rather than as a canonical hadith, its clauses should be weighed as historical material at that level.

   > "The Jews of Banu Awf are one community with the believers; the Jews have their religion and the Muslims have theirs." — *Sīra*: Ibn Ishaq, trans. A. Guillaume, pp. 231–233[8](#ref-8){: .footnote}[19](#ref-19){: .footnote}[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[30](#ref-30){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}[53](#ref-53){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. At Badr, the first major battle after years of persecution, expulsion, and escalating conflict, the reports portray Prophet Muhammad ﷺ praying intensely for help rather than treating victory as guaranteed.

   > "O God, I ask You to fulfill Your promise and covenant." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3953[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. After the military defeat at Uhud, the Qur’an instructed Prophet Muhammad ﷺ to remain gentle with his followers, pardon them, and continue consulting them despite the serious consequences of their mistake.

   > "So by mercy from Allah, [O Muhammad], you were lenient with them. And if you had been rude [in speech] and harsh in heart, they would have disbanded from about you. So pardon them and ask forgiveness for them and consult them in the matter. And when you have decided, then rely upon Allah. Indeed, Allah loves those who rely [upon Him]." (**Sūrat Āl 'Imrān 3:159**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿فَبِمَا رَحْمَةٍ مِّنَ ٱللَّهِ لِنتَ لَهُمْ ۖ وَلَوْ كُنتَ فَظًّا غَلِيظَ ٱلْقَلْبِ لَٱنفَضُّوا۟ مِنْ حَوْلِكَ ۖ فَٱعْفُ عَنْهُمْ وَٱسْتَغْفِرْ لَهُمْ وَشَاوِرْهُمْ فِى ٱلْأَمْرِ ۖ فَإِذَا عَزَمْتَ فَتَوَكَّلْ عَلَى ٱللَّهِ ۚ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ يُحِبُّ ٱلْمُتَوَكِّلِينَ ۝١٥٩﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ آلِ عِمۡرَانَ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. During the Battle of the Trench, Jabir observed Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ severe hunger and prepared food for him, placing the leader of Medina within the community’s deprivation during the siege rather than outside it in materially secure conditions.

   > Jabir reported seeing the Prophet ﷺ suffering from "severe hunger." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4101[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. At Hudaybiyyah in 628, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ approached Mecca intending pilgrimage rather than battle and, after being prevented from entering, repeatedly pursued a negotiated truce with Quraysh.

   > "We have not come to fight anyone. We have come to perform the pilgrimage." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2731–2732[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. During the Hudaybiyyah negotiations, the Meccan representative refused to accept the title “Messenger of God” in the written treaty. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ maintained the claim verbally but accepted the wording “Muhammad son of Abdullah” in order to conclude the agreement.

   > "By God, I am the Messenger of God even if you do not believe me. Write: Muhammad son of Abdullah." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2731–2732[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ accepted treaty terms at Hudaybiyyah that many companions found painful, including a clause requiring certain Meccan escapees to be returned. When Abu Jandal arrived in chains during the negotiations, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ honored the concluded commitment rather than abandoning it in response to the immediate outrage.

   > When Quraysh later demanded compliance, the report says: "Abide by the promise you gave us," and Muhammad ﷺ did so. — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2731–2732[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. When discouraged companions did not immediately follow Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ instruction to end the pilgrimage state after Hudaybiyyah, Umm Salama advised him to begin the rites himself without further argument. He followed her advice, and the companions then followed his example.

   > Umm Salama advised him to go out, sacrifice, and shave without further argument; "the Prophet ﷺ went out" and did so. — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2731–2732[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an described the apparently disappointing Hudaybiyyah truce as a “clear victory,” and the opportunities created by the subsequent peace help explain why early Muslims later treated the agreement as a major turning point.

   > "Indeed, We have given you, [O Muhammad], a clear conquest." (**Sūrat al-Fatḥ 48:1**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿إِنَّا فَتَحْنَا لَكَ فَتْحًا مُّبِينًا ۝١﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الفَتۡحِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. After the conflict at Khaybar, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ allowed Jewish cultivators to continue working the land in return for a share of its produce, preserving an economic relationship despite the preceding military conflict.

   > "The Prophet ﷺ gave Khaybar to the Jews to work and cultivate it, and they would receive half of its produce." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4248[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. When Mecca came under Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ control in 630, he rejected a commander’s declaration that it would be a day of great fighting and publicly reaffirmed the sanctuary’s protection from bloodshed.

   > "It is not lawful for one who believes in God and the Last Day to shed blood in it." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4295[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Early biography records Prophet Muhammad ﷺ granting broad clemency to the defeated Quraysh when Mecca fell. The familiar account comes from *sira* [early biography] rather than Bukhari or Muslim, so the difference in source strength should remain explicit.

   > "Go, for you are free." — *Sīra*: Ibn Hisham's recension of Ibn Ishaq, trans. A. Guillaume, p. 553[8](#ref-8){: .footnote}[19](#ref-19){: .footnote}[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ joined demanding communal labor personally, carrying building materials during construction of the Medina mosque and, according to reports of the Trench, digging alongside the community rather than only supervising others.

   > "The Prophet ﷺ himself began carrying the unbaked bricks for its building." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3906[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sahih al-Bukhari* 6414[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Sahih Muslim reports that eighty armed men who descended on Prophet Muhammad ﷺ and his companions near Mecca were captured and then released rather than executed in retaliation.

   > "Eighty men from the people of Mecca came down upon the Messenger of God armed... He captured them and spared them." — *Sahih Muslim* 1808[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. When Prophet Muhammad ﷺ entered Mecca with overwhelming military strength, civilians were given simple ways to remain safe, including staying inside their own homes rather than being treated as combatants by default.

   > "Whoever enters Abu Sufyan's house is safe; whoever lays down his arms is safe; whoever closes his door is safe." — *Sahih Muslim* 1780c[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. After Hunayn, when a delegation from the defeated Hawazin came seeking their captured families, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ supported returning the captives and urged the Muslim army to release them.

   > "Your brothers have come to you repentant, and I consider that their captives should be returned to them." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4318–4319[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. When victory came, the Qur’an instructed Prophet Muhammad ﷺ to praise God and seek forgiveness, directing success toward worship and self-accounting rather than personal glorification.

   > "When the victory of Allah has come and the conquest, and you see the people entering into the religion of Allah in multitudes, then exalt [Him] with praise of your Lord and ask forgiveness of Him. Indeed, He is ever Accepting of repentance." (**Sūrat an-Naṣr 110:1-3**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿إِذَا جَآءَ نَصْرُ ٱللَّهِ وَٱلْفَتْحُ ۝١ وَرَأَيْتَ ٱلنَّاسَ يَدْخُلُونَ فِى دِينِ ٱللَّهِ أَفْوَاجًا ۝٢ فَسَبِّحْ بِحَمْدِ رَبِّكَ وَٱسْتَغْفِرْهُ ۚ إِنَّهُۥ كَانَ تَوَّابًۢا ۝٣﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ النَّصۡرِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Early biography records a Christian delegation from Najran being received in Medina for sustained religious discussion and reports that they were allowed to perform their own prayer while there. Because this material belongs to *sira* [early biography] rather than the two canonical Sahih collections, the source distinction should remain clear.

   > Reported in the *Sīra* of Ibn Ishaq/Ibn Hisham, and also by Ibn Sa'd, *al-Ṭabaqāt*[8](#ref-8){: .footnote}[9](#ref-9){: .footnote}[19](#ref-19){: .footnote}[20](#ref-20){: .footnote}[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[29](#ref-29){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}.
   {: .quote }

1. Reports preserve Prophet Muhammad ﷺ sending invitations to worship one God beyond Arabia’s tribal audience. Bukhari preserves the letter to the Byzantine emperor Heraclius within Abu Sufyan’s account, while early historical sources preserve letters associated with other neighboring rulers.

   > "I invite you with the invitation of Islam..." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 7[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sīra*: early biography, for the wider correspondence[8](#ref-8){: .footnote}[9](#ref-9){: .footnote}[30](#ref-30){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

### <span class="num-alpha">D.</span> The final pilgrimage and death
{: #the-final-pilgrimage-and-death }

1. During his final pilgrimage, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ told the gathered community that one another’s lives and property were sacred, reaffirming those protections near the end of his life.

   > "Your blood and your property are sacred to one another, like the sacredness of this day, in this month, in this town." — *Sahih Muslim* 1218a[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. When Prophet Muhammad ﷺ abolished outstanding pre-Islamic usury claims, he began with a financial claim belonging to his own family rather than shielding relatives from the reform.

   > "The usury of the pre-Islamic period is abolished, and the first usury I abolish is that of Abbas ibn Abd al-Muttalib." — *Sahih Muslim* 1218a[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. When Prophet Muhammad ﷺ abolished inherited claims of blood revenge, he likewise began with a claim connected to his own clan.

   > "The blood claims of the pre-Islamic period are abolished, and the first blood claim I abolish is one of ours." — *Sahih Muslim* 1218a[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. In the final pilgrimage, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ reminded men that women had rights to proper provision and decent treatment within marriage.

   > "Be mindful of God concerning women... Their right over you is that you provide for them and clothe them in a fitting manner." — *Sahih Muslim* 1218a[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. In the same final sermon, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ asked the people whether he had delivered the message entrusted to him, presenting his task as conveying revelation rather than securing personal immortality or dynastic rule.

   > They replied, "We bear witness that you conveyed, fulfilled, and gave sincere counsel." He said, "O God, bear witness." — *Sahih Muslim* 1218a[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Near his death, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ privately told Fatima that he believed his end was approaching; the report preserves her first weeping at what he told her and then laughing after he gave her a second confidence.

   > "I think my death has drawn near, and you will be the first of my family to follow me." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3623–3624[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. According to Aisha, Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ final words were directed toward God and his desire for the highest companionship with Him, not toward money, office, or succession.

   > "O God, with the Highest Companion." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4463[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an had already told the community that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was a mortal messenger who could die, so his death was not allowed to become a denial of his humanity or a reason for the faith itself to collapse.

   > "Muhammad is not but a messenger. [Other] messengers have passed on before him. So if he was to die or be killed, would you turn back on your heels [to unbelief]? And he who turns back on his heels will never harm Allah at all; but Allah will reward the grateful." (**Sūrat Āl 'Imrān 3:144**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَمَا مُحَمَّدٌ إِلَّا رَسُولٌ قَدْ خَلَتْ مِن قَبْلِهِ ٱلرُّسُلُ ۚ أَفَإِي۟ن مَّاتَ أَوْ قُتِلَ ٱنقَلَبْتُمْ عَلَىٰٓ أَعْقَـٰبِكُمْ ۚ وَمَن يَنقَلِبْ عَلَىٰ عَقِبَيْهِ فَلَن يَضُرَّ ٱللَّهَ شَيْـًٔا ۗ وَسَيَجْزِى ٱللَّهُ ٱلشَّـٰكِرِينَ ۝١٤٤﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ آلِ عِمۡرَانَ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ died without a royal treasury for his descendants: his armor was still pledged for food, and he had taught that what prophets leave as property is to be treated as charity rather than dynastic inheritance.

   > "We prophets are not inherited from. What we leave is charity." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4240–4241[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. In his final public teaching, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ warned his followers not to turn against one another in bloodshed after his death.

   > "Do not return after me to striking one another's necks." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 7078[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Reports from Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ final illness remember him repeatedly emphasizing prayer and responsibility toward people under one’s authority rather than focusing his final instructions on preserving a family dynasty or private estate.

   > "The prayer, the prayer, and those whom your right hands possess." — *Sunan Abi Dawud* 5156[4](#ref-4){: .footnote}[15](#ref-15){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sunan Ibn Majah* 2698, graded sound[6](#ref-6){: .footnote}[17](#ref-17){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

### <span class="num-alpha">E.</span> The society and generation he left behind
{: #the-society-and-generation-he-left-behind }

1. Over roughly twenty-three years, Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ movement grew from a persecuted preaching community without a state into a polity covering most of Arabia, with institutions of worship, law, taxation, charity, treaty-making, learning, and written administration. The speed and scale of that historical change do not by themselves prove revelation, but any account of his career has to explain them.

   > "It is He who has sent among the unlettered a Messenger from themselves reciting to them His verses and purifying them and teaching them the Book and wisdom - although they were before in clear error -" (**Sūrat al-Jumu'ah 62:2**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿هُوَ ٱلَّذِى بَعَثَ فِى ٱلْأُمِّيِّـۧنَ رَسُولًا مِّنْهُمْ يَتْلُوا۟ عَلَيْهِمْ ءَايَـٰتِهِۦ وَيُزَكِّيهِمْ وَيُعَلِّمُهُمُ ٱلْكِتَـٰبَ وَٱلْحِكْمَةَ وَإِن كَانُوا۟ مِن قَبْلُ لَفِى ضَلَـٰلٍ مُّبِينٍ ۝٢﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الجُمُعَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Some demanding reforms appear in the sources as taking practical effect quickly even without a large enforcement bureaucracy. Anas remembered wine being poured away when prohibition was announced, while the Farewell Pilgrimage abolished financial and blood claims beginning with claims tied to Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ own family. This does not imply that every social vice disappeared immediately, but it shows that costly norms could acquire real communal force.

   > Anas reported that when the prohibition of wine was announced, the containers in Medina were emptied. — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2464[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4620[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sahih Muslim* 1218a[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The Medinan tribes Aws and Khazraj had fought through generations of rivalry, including the battle of Bu’ath shortly before Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ arrival. The Qur’an later appealed to their own memory of having once been enemies whose hearts had been brought together.

   > "And hold firmly to the rope of Allah all together and do not become divided. And remember the favor of Allah upon you - when you were enemies and He brought your hearts together and you became, by His favor, brothers. And you were on the edge of a pit of the Fire, and He saved you from it. Thus does Allah make clear to you His verses that you may be guided." (**Sūrat Āl 'Imrān 3:103**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}; *Sīra*: early biography, for the Bu'ath background[8](#ref-8){: .footnote}[19](#ref-19){: .footnote}[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَٱعْتَصِمُوا۟ بِحَبْلِ ٱللَّهِ جَمِيعًا وَلَا تَفَرَّقُوا۟ ۚ وَٱذْكُرُوا۟ نِعْمَتَ ٱللَّهِ عَلَيْكُمْ إِذْ كُنتُمْ أَعْدَآءً فَأَلَّفَ بَيْنَ قُلُوبِكُمْ فَأَصْبَحْتُم بِنِعْمَتِهِۦٓ إِخْوَٰنًا وَكُنتُمْ عَلَىٰ شَفَا حُفْرَةٍ مِّنَ ٱلنَّارِ فَأَنقَذَكُم مِّنْهَا ۗ كَذَٰلِكَ يُبَيِّنُ ٱللَّهُ لَكُمْ ءَايَـٰتِهِۦ لَعَلَّكُمْ تَهْتَدُونَ ۝١٠٣﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ آلِ عِمۡرَانَ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The new Medinan community did more than admit refugees from Mecca: early biography records practical bonds created between emigrant and Medinan households, while the Qur’an praises the hosts for preferring the newcomers even when they themselves were in need.

   > "They give [them] preference over themselves, even though they are in privation." (**Sūrat al-Ḥashr 59:9**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}; *Sīra*: early biography, for the pairing of emigrants and helpers[8](#ref-8){: .footnote}[19](#ref-19){: .footnote}[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَٱلَّذِينَ تَبَوَّءُو ٱلدَّارَ وَٱلْإِيمَـٰنَ مِن قَبْلِهِمْ يُحِبُّونَ مَنْ هَاجَرَ إِلَيْهِمْ وَلَا يَجِدُونَ فِى صُدُورِهِمْ حَاجَةً مِّمَّآ أُوتُوا۟ وَيُؤْثِرُونَ عَلَىٰٓ أَنفُسِهِمْ وَلَوْ كَانَ بِهِمْ خَصَاصَةٌ ۚ وَمَن يُوقَ شُحَّ نَفْسِهِۦ فَأُو۟لَـٰٓئِكَ هُمُ ٱلْمُفْلِحُونَ ۝٩﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الحَشۡرِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. After Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ death, the first generation did not turn him into a divine figure. Early reports remember Abu Bakr calming the community by reciting the Qur’anic reminder that Muhammad ﷺ was a mortal messenger, after which that generation continued as administrators, judges, teachers, and transmitters rather than custodians of a royal cult centered on him.

   > "Muhammad is not but a messenger. [Other] messengers have passed on before him. So if he was to die or be killed, would you turn back on your heels [to unbelief]?" (**Sūrat Āl 'Imrān 3:144**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}; recited by Abu Bakr in his address after Muhammad's ﷺ death, reported in *Sahih al-Bukhari*
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَمَا مُحَمَّدٌ إِلَّا رَسُولٌ قَدْ خَلَتْ مِن قَبْلِهِ ٱلرُّسُلُ ۚ أَفَإِي۟ن مَّاتَ أَوْ قُتِلَ ٱنقَلَبْتُمْ عَلَىٰٓ أَعْقَـٰبِكُمْ ۚ وَمَن يَنقَلِبْ عَلَىٰ عَقِبَيْهِ فَلَن يَضُرَّ ٱللَّهَ شَيْـًٔا ۗ وَسَيَجْزِى ٱللَّهُ ٱلشَّـٰكِرِينَ ۝١٤٤﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ آلِ عِمۡرَانَ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

---

## <span class="num-roman">IV.</span> Why his claim to prophethood deserves serious attention
{: #why-his-claim-to-prophethood-deserves-serious-attention }

The case for Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ prophethood is strongest when its parts are considered together. **Good character alone does not prove revelation;** a sincere person can still be mistaken. **A prediction can be ambiguous,** a miracle report is only as useful as its transmission, and **literary power is partly a matter of judgment**. The evidence therefore should not be made to carry more weight individually than it can bear. The relevant question is which overall explanation best accounts for the man, the text he delivered, the risks he accepted, the advantages he refused to exploit, the predictions and miracles attributed to him, and the responses of people who knew him closely.

### <span class="num-alpha">A.</span> Character, incentives, and behavior that are difficult to fit to a cynical fraud
{: #character-incentives-and-behavior-that-are-difficult-to-fit-to-a-cynical-fraud }

1. Bukhari preserves Abu Sufyan, while still an opponent of Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, telling the Byzantine emperor Heraclius that Muhammad ﷺ had not been known as a liar before claiming prophethood.

   > Heraclius asked, "Had you ever accused him of lying before he said what he now says?" Abu Sufyan replied, "No." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 7[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The same hostile witness told Heraclius that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was not known for breaking agreements, and Abu Sufyan later acknowledged that he would have preferred to say something damaging if he could.

   > Asked whether Muhammad ﷺ broke covenants, Abu Sufyan said, "No." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 7[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Abu Sufyan described Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ early followers as largely socially weak while also saying that their numbers were increasing. This does not establish revelation, but it weighs against a simple picture of a movement initially built by flattering the existing elite.

   > Asked whether his followers were the nobles or the weak, Abu Sufyan replied, "Rather, the weak." Asked whether they increased, he said, "They increase." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 7[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. When Heraclius asked what Prophet Muhammad ﷺ taught, Abu Sufyan summarized the message as worshipping God alone, abandoning ancestral idols, praying, speaking truthfully, remaining chaste, and maintaining family ties rather than centering it on devotion to Muhammad ﷺ himself.

   > "He tells us to worship God alone... and he orders us to pray, be truthful, remain chaste, and keep family ties." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 7[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Khadija had known Prophet Muhammad ﷺ privately for years before the public prophetic mission. After the first revelation, she reassured him by appealing to the character she had already observed and concluded that God would not disgrace a person who lived that way.

   > "You keep family ties, support the weak, help the destitute, honor the guest, and assist those struck by calamity." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Anas served Prophet Muhammad ﷺ for ten years after the prophetic mission had begun and did not remember the kind of petty domination or constant irritation that would be easy for a carefully managed public persona to conceal from outsiders but difficult to hide in daily household life.

   > "I served the Prophet ﷺ for ten years. He never said 'uff' [an expression of irritation] to me." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 6038[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Aisha’s testimony from inside the household adds another intimate check: she reported that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ never struck a woman or a servant.

   > "The Messenger of God ﷺ never struck anyone with his hand, neither a woman nor a servant." — *Sahih Muslim* 2328a[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. For much of the Meccan period, Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ claim brought hostility, insecurity, persecution of his followers, loss of protection, rejection at al-Ta’if, and eventual migration rather than political office or material comfort. A theory based on immediate personal gain has to account for that prolonged cost.

   > "If you do not aid the Prophet - Allah has already aided him when those who disbelieved had driven him out [of Makkah] as one of two, when they were in the cave and he said to his companion, 'Do not grieve; indeed Allah is with us.' And Allah sent down his tranquillity upon him and supported him with angels you did not see and made the word of those who disbelieved the lowest, while the word of Allah - that is the highest. And Allah is Exalted in Might and Wise." (**Sūrat al-Tawbah 9:40**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿إِلَّا تَنصُرُوهُ فَقَدْ نَصَرَهُ ٱللَّهُ إِذْ أَخْرَجَهُ ٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟ ثَانِىَ ٱثْنَيْنِ إِذْ هُمَا فِى ٱلْغَارِ إِذْ يَقُولُ لِصَـٰحِبِهِۦ لَا تَحْزَنْ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ مَعَنَا ۖ فَأَنزَلَ ٱللَّهُ سَكِينَتَهُۥ عَلَيْهِ وَأَيَّدَهُۥ بِجُنُودٍ لَّمْ تَرَوْهَا وَجَعَلَ كَلِمَةَ ٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟ ٱلسُّفْلَىٰ ۗ وَكَلِمَةُ ٱللَّهِ هِىَ ٱلْعُلْيَا ۗ وَٱللَّهُ عَزِيزٌ حَكِيمٌ ۝٤٠﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ التَّوۡبَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. By the end of his life Prophet Muhammad ﷺ led most of Arabia, yet reports still describe his household going long periods without cooked food and his armor remaining pledged for food at death. Those details fit poorly with an explanation centered on accumulating a private royal fortune.

   > "The Messenger of God ﷺ died while his iron armor was pledged to a Jewish man for thirty measures of barley." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2916[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ also limited obvious avenues for dynastic enrichment: he taught that prophetic property would pass to charity rather than heirs, barred his household from obligatory alms, made his grandson return a charity date, and created no hereditary throne for a relative after all of his sons had died in childhood.

   > "We prophets are not inherited from. What we leave is charity." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4240-4241[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sahih al-Bukhari* 1491[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sahih Muslim* 1072a[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ explicitly prohibited followers from exaggerating his status through forms of praise that would elevate him beyond servanthood and prophethood.

   > "Do not exaggerate in praising me... I am only a servant. Say: God’s servant and His Messenger." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3445[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }
